Although [decriminalised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodomy_law), [homosexuality](5488304.md) remains [stigmatised in Iraqi society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homophobia).[^cite_note-183] [Human rights in Islamic State-controlled territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Islamic_State-controlled_territory) have been recorded as highly violated. It included [mass executions in Islamic State-occupied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_executions_in_Islamic_State-occupied_Mosul) part of Mosul and [genocide of the Yazidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazidi_genocide) in Yazidi populated [Sinjar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinjar), which is in northern Iraq.[^cite_note-184]

### Administrative divisions

Main articles: [Federal regions of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_regions_of_Iraq), [Governorates of Iraq](222800.md), [Districts of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_Iraq), and [Subdistricts of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subdistricts_of_Iraq)

In terms of administrative divisions, Iraq is currently composed of a single [federal region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_regions_of_Iraq) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): *iqlīm ittihādi*) and nineteen [governorates](222800.md) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): *muhafadhat*, singular *muhafadhah*). The governorates are divided into [districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_Iraq) (or [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): *aqdhiyah*, singular *qadhā*), which are further divided into [sub-districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subdistricts_of_Iraq) (or [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): *nawāḥī*, singular *nāhiyah*). There are 120 districts and 394 subdistricts in Iraq.&#x20;

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Iraq)



Historical economic growth of Iraq

According to the [International Fund for Agricultural Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IFAD), Iraq is an [oil-rich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil-rich_country) upper-middle-income country.[^cite_note-IFAD-2025-185] Iraq's economy is dominated by the [oil](23195.md) sector, which has traditionally provided about 95% of foreign exchange earnings.[^cite_note-IFAD-2025-185] The lack of development in other sectors has resulted in 18%–30% unemployed and a per capita GDP of \$4,812.[^cite_note-cia-3][^cite_note-IFAD-2025-185] Public sector employment accounted for nearly 60% of full-time employment in 2011.[^cite_note-usaid11-186] The oil export industry, which dominates the Iraqi economy, generates little employment.[^cite_note-usaid11-186] Currently only a modest percentage of women (the highest estimate for 2011 was 22%) participate in the labour force.[^cite_note-usaid11-186] The official currency in Iraq is the [Iraqi dinar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_dinar). The Central Provisional Authority issued new dinar coins and notes, with the notes printed by [De La Rue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_La_Rue) using modern anti-forgery techniques.[^cite_note-187] [Jim Cramer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Cramer)'s 20 October 2009 endorsement of the [Iraqi dinar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_dinar) on [CNBC](223441.md) has further piqued interest in the investment.[^cite_note-188]



Erbil skyline at night

Prior to the 2003 invasion, Iraq's [centrally planned economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_planning) prohibited the foreign ownership of businesses, ran most large industries as state-owned enterprises, and imposed large [tariffs](55551.md) to keep the foreign goods out.[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-Tooze-2023-190] Oil was nationalised in 1972 and its revenue was spent on government development projects. Iraq was one of the most advanced countries in the Middle East. But it faced economic decline as a result of sanctions. After [2003](201936.md), the [Coalition Provisional Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_Provisional_Authority) quickly began issuing many binding orders [privatising](24661.md) the Iraqi economy and opening it up to [foreign investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment).[^cite_note-Tooze-2023-190] On 20 November 2004, the [Paris Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Club) of creditor countries agreed to write off 80% (\$33 billion) of Iraq's \$42 billion debt to Club members. Iraq's total external debt was around \$120 billion at the time of the invasion, and had grown another \$5 billion by 2004. The [debt relief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debt_relief) was to be implemented in three stages: two of 30% each and one of 20%.[^cite_note-191]

Five years after the invasion, an estimated 2.4 million people were [internally displaced](427408.md) (with a further two million refugees outside Iraq), four million Iraqis were considered food-insecure (a quarter of children were chronically malnourished) and only a third of Iraqi children had access to safe drinking water.[^cite_note-ODI1-192] In 2022, and after more than 30 years after the [UN Compensation Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Compensation_Commission) was created to ensure restitution for Kuwait following the invasion of 1990, the reparations body announced that Iraq has paid a total of \$52.4 billion in war reparations to Kuwait.[^cite_note-193] According to the Overseas Development Institute, [international NGOs](4142591.md) face challenges in carrying out their mission, leaving their assistance "piecemeal and largely conducted undercover, hindered by insecurity, a lack of coordinated funding, limited operational capacity and patchy information".[^cite_note-ODI1-192] International NGOs have been targeted and during the first 5 years, 94 aid workers were killed, 248 injured, 24 arrested or detained and 89 kidnapped or abducted.[^cite_note-ODI1-192]



The Amwaj Residential Towers in Baghdad, a modern high-rise complex that represents part of the city's recent urban development projects.

The economy has been heavily affected by the war.[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195] According to a report by the [Arab News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_News), Iraq has shown positive signs of recovery.[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197] The Kurdish and Shia populated regions of Iraq experienced an economic boom after the end of the war,[^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199][^cite_note-alarabiya.net-200] and until 2023,[^cite_note-Conv-201][^cite_note-NSI-202] Kurdistan Region was considered economically more stable —mostly driven by hitherto independent oil exports.[^cite_note-203] Recent developments in the internal political dynamics of the country has seen Baghdad reassert full control over the oil industry of the country[^cite_note-204] and has been since considered more stable and prosperous,[^cite_note-205] while Kurdistan Region has experienced an economic downfall.[^cite_note-Conv-201][^cite_note-NSI-202] In recent years, Sunni-populated provinces in Iraq have also made economic progress, as evidenced by numerous new construction projects.[^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208] In 2025, parliament speaker [Mahmoud al-Mashhadani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_al-Mashhadani) stressed that Iraq is stable in terms of security and economy and has taken a non-aligned approach.[^cite_note-209] According to a new report from the Arab Investment & Export Credit Guarantee Corporation ("Dhaman"), the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md), Saudi Arabia, [Egypt](8087628.md), Iraq, and [Algeria](358.md), the leading contributors to the [Arab economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_Arab_League) and 72% of the region's GDP.[^cite_note-210][^cite_note-211] In addition, Iraq is an agricultural country.[^cite_note-212] Tourism in Iraq stands to be a major growth sector, including [archaeological tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Iraq) and [religious tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_tourism) while the country is also considered to be a potential location for [ecotourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecotourism).[^cite_note-213][^cite_note-214][^cite_note-215]

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Iraq)



A reconstructed portion of the ruins of [Babylon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylon)

Iraq was an important [tourist destination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourist_destination) for many years but that changed dramatically during the [war with Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War) and after the [invasion by the US and allies](201936.md).[^cite_note-216] As Iraq continues to develop and stabilises, tourism in Iraq is still facing many challenges, and little has been made by the government to meet its tremendous potential as a global tourist destination, and gain the associated economic benefits, mainly due to conflicts.[^cite_note-217] Sites from Iraq's ancient past are numerous and many that are close to large cities have been excavated. Babylon has seen major recent restoration; known for its famous [*Ziggurat*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ziggurat_of_Ur) (the inspiration for the Biblical [Tower of Babel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tower_of_Babel)), the [Hanging Gardens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanging_Gardens_of_Babylon) (one of the [Seven Wonders of the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wonders_of_the_World)), and the [Ishtar Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ishtar_Gate), making it a prime destination.

[Nineveh](21699.md), a rival to [Babylon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylon), has also seen significant restoration and reconstruction.[^cite_note-Iraq-218] [Ur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ur), one of the first Sumerian cities, which is near [Nasiriyya](33572510.md), has been partially restored.[^cite_note-Iraq-218] This is a list of examples of some significant sites in a country with a tremendous archaeological and historic wealth.[^cite_note-Iraq-218] Iraq is considered to be a potential location for [ecotourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecotourism).[^cite_note-219] The tourism in Iraq includes also making pilgrimages to [holy Shia Islamic sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holiest_sites_in_Islam_\(Shia\)) near [Karbala](210957.md) and [Najaf](200512.md).[^cite_note-alarabiya.net-200] Since 2003, Najaf and Karbala have experienced economic boom, due to religious tourism.[^cite_note-alarabiya.net-200]

Saddam Hussein built hundreds of palaces and monuments across the country. Some of them include [Al-Faw Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Faw_Palace), [As-Salam Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/As-Salam_Palace) and [Radwaniyah Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radwaniyah_Palace).[^cite_note-Vince-2016-220] Al-Faw Palace is currently occupied by the [American University of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_University_of_Iraq_-_Baghdad). Since Saddam's overthrow, the palaces are open to tourists, though they are not officially functioning, and the [government of Iraq](11752991.md) is considering to sell them for useful purposes. A majority of these structures were built after the [1991 Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War), when Iraq was put under sanctions by the United Nations.[^cite_note-Vince-2016-220] Saddam reconstructed part of Babylon, one of the world's earliest cities, using bricks inscribed with his name to associate himself with the region's past glories.[^cite_note-221] One of his palaces in Basra was turned into a museum, despite it was time when Iraq allied with the US was engaged in war with the ISIS.[^cite_note-222]

### Transport

Main article: [Transport in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Iraq)



The [150 Meter Motorway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motorway_150_\(Iraq\)) is [Erbil](24534207.md)'s largest ring road, built to improve traffic flow and connect expanding areas of the city.

Iraq has a modern network of motorways. [Roadways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roads_in_Iraq) extended 45,550 km (28,300 mi).[^cite_note-sites.google.com-223] The roadway also connect Iraq to neighbouring countries of [Iran](14653.md), [Turkey](11125639.md), [Syria](7515849.md), [Jordan](7515964.md), [Saudi Arabia](349303.md) and [Kuwait](7515890.md).[^cite_note-sites.google.com-223] There are more than seven million passenger cars, over million commercial taxis, buses, and trucks in use. On major motorways the maximum speed is 110 km/h (68 mph).[^cite_note-224] Many of the roads were constructed in the late 1970s and early 1980s and were designed with a 20-year lifespan.[^cite_note-ITA-2021-225] Most of these facilities were damaged in enduring wars, that Iraq experienced.[^cite_note-ITA-2021-225] Since then traffic has been a serious issue, specially in Baghdad.

[Iraqi Republic Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Republic_Railways) is the responsible body for railway transportation in Iraq.[^cite_note-Iraq-2011-226] The railway infrastructure consists of 2,405 km (1,494 mi) of track, 109 stations, 31 locomotives and 1,685 units of rolling stock.[^cite_note-Iraq-2011-226] The government is attempting to establish railway links with Turkey, Kuwait, and Saudi Arabia to complete a continuous Euro-Gulf rail route.[^cite_note-Iraq-2011-226] Currently, a large project is underway to connect Karbala and Najaf.

Most of Iraq's oil exports are done through its ports.[^cite_note-ITA-2021-225] Basra is the only coastal governorate of Iraq.[^cite_note-ITA-2021-225] It is home to all of Iraq's six ports — [Abu Flous Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Flous_Port), [Al Başrah Oil Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Ba%C5%9Frah_Oil_Terminal), [Grand Faw Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Faw_Port), [Khor Al Amaya Oil Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khor_Al_Amaya_Oil_Terminal), [Khor Al Zubair Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khor_Al_Zubair_Port), [Port of Basra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Basra) and [Umm Qasr Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_Qasr_Port).[^cite_note-ITA-2021-225] Iraq has about 104 airports as of 2012.[^cite_note-Iraq-2011-226] Major airports at [Baghdad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baghdad_International_Airport), [Basra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basra_International_Airport), [Erbil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erbil_International_Airport), [Sulaymaniyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulaimaniyah_International_Airport), [Kirkuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirkuk_International_Airport) and [Najaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Najaf_International_Airport).[^cite_note-Iraq-2011-226] The government is constructing international airports for [Karbala](210957.md) and [Nasiriyah](33572510.md). [Nasiriyah Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasiriyah_Airport) is in partnership with China and reoping of Mosul Airport, which was closed during the 2013–2017 civil war.[^cite_note-Iraq-2011-226][^cite_note-227][^cite_note-228][^cite_note-Frbiu_03-2017-229]

### Oil and energy

Main articles: [Oil reserves in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_reserves_in_Iraq) and [Energy in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Iraq)



[Khawr Al Amaya Oil Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khawr_Al_Amaya_Oil_Terminal)

With its 143.1 billion barrels (2.275×10<sup>10</sup> m<sup>3</sup>) of proved oil reserves, Iraq ranks third in the world behind Venezuela and Saudi Arabia in the amount of [oil reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_reserves).[^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231] Oil production levels reached 3.4 million barrels per day by December 2012.[^cite_note-232] Only about 2,000 [oil wells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_well) have been drilled in Iraq, compared with about 1 million wells in [Texas](29810.md) alone.[^cite_note-233] Iraq was one of the founding members of [OPEC](166346.md).[^cite_note-234][^cite_note-235]

During the 1970s Iraq produced up to 3.5 million [barrels per day](594043.md), but [sanctions imposed against Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctions_against_Iraq) after its [invasion of Kuwait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Kuwait) in 1990 crippled the country's oil sector. The [sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_against_Iraq) prohibited Iraq from exporting oil until 1996 and Iraq's output declined by 85% in the years following the First [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War). The sanctions were lifted in 2003 after the US-led invasion removed Saddam Hussein from power, but development of Iraq's oil resources has been hampered by the ongoing conflict.[^cite_note-atlantic-236] As of 2010, despite improved security and billions of dollars in oil revenue, Iraq still generates about half the electricity that customers demand, leading to protests during the hot summer months.[^cite_note-237] The [Iraq oil law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_oil_law_\(2007\)), a proposed piece of legislation submitted to the [Council of Representatives of Iraq](1445450.md) in 2007, has failed to gain approval due to disagreements among Iraq's various political blocs.[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239] [Al Başrah Oil Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Ba%C5%9Frah_Oil_Terminal) is a trans-shipment facility from the pipelines to the tankers and uses [supertankers](15923603.md).

According to a US Study from May 2007, between 100,000 barrels per day (16,000 m<sup>3</sup>/d) and 300,000 barrels per day (48,000 m<sup>3</sup>/d) of Iraq's declared oil production over the past four years could have been siphoned off through corruption or smuggling.[^cite_note-240] In 2008, [Al Jazeera](30204052.md) reported \$13 billion of Iraqi oil revenues in American care was improperly accounted for, of which \$2.6 billion is totally unaccounted for.[^cite_note-241] Some reports that the government has reduced corruption in public procurement of oil; however, reliable reports of bribery and kickbacks to government officials persist.[^cite_note-242]

On 30 June and 11 December 2009, the [Ministry of Oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Oil_\(Iraq\)) awarded service contracts to international oil companies for some of Iraq's many oil fields.[^cite_note-aljazeera091211-243][^cite_note-aljazeera090630-244] Oil fields contracted include the "super-giant" [Majnoon oil field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majnoon_oil_field), [Halfaya Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halfaya_Field), [West Qurna Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Qurna_Field) and [Rumaila Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumaila_oil_field).[^cite_note-aljazeera090630-244] [BP](18998720.md) and [China National Petroleum Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_National_Petroleum_Corporation) won a deal to develop Rumaila, the largest oil field in Iraq.[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246] On 14 March 2014, the [International Energy Agency](285292.md) said Iraq's oil output jumped by half a million barrels a day in February to average 3.6 million barrels a day. The country had not pumped that much oil since 1979, when Saddam Hussein rose to power.[^cite_note-247] However, on 14 July 2014, as sectarian strife had taken hold, [Kurdistan Regional Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdistan_Regional_Government) forces seized control of the Bai Hassan and [Kirkuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirkuk_Field) oilfields in the north of the country, taking them from Iraq's control. Baghdad condemned the seizure and threatened "dire consequences" if the fields were not returned.[^cite_note-KurdsOilfields-248] On 2018, the UN estimated that oil accounts for 99% of Iraq's revenue.[^cite_note-atlantic-236] As of 2021, the oil sector provided about 92% of foreign exchange earnings.[^cite_note-249]

### Water supply and sanitation

Main article: [Water supply and sanitation in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_Iraq)



[Lake Dukan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Dukan)

Three decades of war greatly cut the existing [water resources management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_resource_management) system for several major cities. This prompted widespread [water supply](684928.md) and [sanitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanitation) shortfalls thus poor [water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_quality) and service quality.[^cite_note-watercrisis2-134] This is combined with few businesses and households who are fully environmentally aware and legally compliant however the large lakes, as pictured, alleviate supply relative to many comparators in Western Asia beset by more regular drought. Access to potable water diverges among governorates and between urban and rural areas. 91% of the population has access to potable water. Forming this figure: in rural areas, 77% of people have access to improved (treated or fully naturally filtered) drinking water sources; and 98% in urban areas.[^cite_note-UNWater-250] Much water is discarded during treatment, due to much outmoded equipment, raising energy burden and reducing supply.[^cite_note-UNWater-250]

### Infrastructure

Main article: [Investment in post-invasion Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Investment_in_post-invasion_Iraq)

Although many infrastructure projects had already begun, at the end of 2013 Iraq had a housing crisis. The then war-ravaged country was set to complete 5 percent of the 2.5 million homes it needs to build by 2016 to keep up with demand, confirmed the Minister for Construction and Housing.[^cite_note-251] In 2009, the Iraq Britain Business Council formed. Its key impetus was House of Lords member and trade expert [Lady Nicholson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emma_Nicholson,_Baroness_Nicholson_of_Winterbourne). In 2013, South Korean firm Daewoo reached a deal to build [Bismayah New City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bismayah_New_City) of about 600,000 residents in 100,000 homes.[^cite_note-252]

In December 2020, [Al-Sudani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Shia'_Al_Sudani) launched the second phase of the [Grand Faw Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Faw_Port) via winning bid of project head contractor [Daewoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daewoo) at \$2.7 billion.[^cite_note-253] In late 2023, the government announced that it will build a total of 15 new cities across Iraq, in an attempt to tackle a persistent housing problem, according to officials.[^cite_note-254] This project falls under the government's plan and strategy to establish new residential cities outside city centres, aiming to alleviate the urban housing crisis.[^cite_note-INA-2024-255] The first 5 new cities will be located in [Baghdad](4492.md), [Babylon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylon_Governorate), [Nineveh](200568.md), [Anbar](467036.md) and [Karbala](210957.md), while another 10 new residential cities will be launched in other governorates.[^cite_note-INA-2024-255] The initial phase of the \[housing] plan began in late 2023, when [Al-Sudani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Shia'_Al_Sudani) laid the foundation stone of Al-Jawahiri city.[^cite_note-INA-2024-255] Located west of the capital, the new city will host 30,000 housing units which will cost \$2 billion.[^cite_note-INA-2024-255] It is expected to be completed in four to five years. According to officials, none of it is financed by the government.[^cite_note-256][^cite_note-iraqinews.com-257][^cite_note-INA-2024-255]

In 2024, and during a visit to Baghdad by Turkish President [Recep Tayyip Erdogan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan), a quadrilateral memorandum of understanding regarding cooperation in the [Iraq–Europe Development Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq%E2%80%93Europe_Development_Road) project was signed between Iraq, [Turkey](11125639.md), [Qatar](8238258.md), [UAE](69328.md). The deal was inked by the transportation ministers from each country. The 1,200-km project with railway and motorways which will connect the [Grand Faw Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Faw_Port), aimed to be the largest port in the Middle East. According to officials, it is a strategic international project which will strengthen Iraq's geopolitical position.[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259][^cite_note-260]

## Demographics

Main articles: [Demographics of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Iraq) and [List of largest cities of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_cities_of_Iraq)

Further information: [Iraqis](4184636.md)

The 2021 estimate of the total Iraqi population is 43,533,592.[^cite_note-UN_WPP-261][^cite_note-UN_WPP_2022-262] Iraq's population was estimated to be 2 million in 1878.[^cite_note-Issawi1988-263] In 2013 Iraq's population reached 35 million amid a post-war population boom.[^cite_note-264] It is the most populous country in the [Arabian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_Plate).[^cite_note-geodatathe-265] Iraq is made up of three former administrative divisions (*vilayets*) of the Ottoman Empire — Mosul, Basra and Baghdad — which were designated as concentration of different ethnic groups.

### Ethnic groups



&#x20; Sunni Arabs

&#x20; Shiite Arabs

&#x20; Sunni Kurds

&#x20; Assyrians

&#x20; Yazidis

&#x20; Turkmen

&#x20;Map of all majority-group clusters of the country's ethnic groups in large, deliberately grouped, census output areas as at the 2006 to 2008 study

Iraq's native population is predominantly [Arab](2185.md), but also includes other ethnic groups such as [Kurds](17068.md), [Turkmens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Turkmen), [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_people), [Yazidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazidis), [Shabaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabaks), [Armenians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenians_in_Iraq), [Mandaeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandaeans), [Circassians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians), and [Kawliya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kawliya).

A report by the [European Parliamentary Research Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Parliamentary_Research_Service) suggests that, in 2015, there were 24 million Arabs (14 million [Shia](26961.md) and 9 million [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni)); 4.7 million Sunni [Kurds](17068.md) (plus 500,000 [Feyli Kurds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feyli_Kurds) and 200,000 [Kaka'i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaka'i)); 3 million (mostly Sunni) [Iraqi Turkmens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Turkmens); 1 million [Black Iraqis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Iraqis); 500,000 [Christians](18337522.md) (including [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrians_in_Iraq) and [Armenians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenians_in_Iraq)); 500,000 [Yazidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazidis); 250,000 [Shabaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabaks); 50,000 [Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gypsies_in_Iraq); 3,000 [Mandaeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandaeans); 2,000 [Circassians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians_in_Iraq); 1,000 of the [Baháʼí Faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith); and a few dozen [Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Iraq).[^cite_note-EPRS-267]

According to the [CIA World Factbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_World_Factbook), citing a 1987 Iraqi government estimate,[^cite_note-cia-3] the population of Iraq is 75–80% [Arab](2185.md) followed by 15–20% [Kurds](35423251.md).[^cite_note-cia-3] In addition, the estimate claims that other minorities form 5% of the country's population, including the [Turkmens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Turkmens), [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_people), [Yezidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yezidis), [Shabak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabak_people), [Kaka'i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaka'i), [Bedouins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bedouins), [Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gypsies_in_Iraq), [Circassians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians_in_Iraq), [Mandaeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandaeans), and [Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranians_in_Iraq).[^cite_note-cia-3] However, the [International Crisis Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Crisis_Group) points out that figures from the 1987 census, as well as the 1967, 1977, and 1997 censuses, "are all considered highly problematic, due to suspicions of regime manipulation" because Iraqi citizens were only allowed to indicate belonging to either the Arab or Kurdish ethnic groups;[^cite_note-ICG-268] consequently, this skewed the number of other ethnic minorities, such as Iraq's third largest ethnic group – the Turkmens.[^cite_note-ICG-268]

The historic Assyrian Quarter in Baghdad housed 150,000 Armenians in 2003. Most of them fled, following the escalation of war, and today only 1,500 Armenians are found in the city. Around 20,000 [Marsh Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marsh_Arabs) live in southern Iraq.[^cite_note-269] Iraq has a community of 2,500 [Chechens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chechen_people),[^cite_note-270] and some 20,000 [Armenians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Armenians).[^cite_note-271] In southern Iraq, [there is a community of Iraqis of African](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro_Iraqis) descent, a legacy of the [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery) practised in the Islamic Caliphate beginning before the [Zanj Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zanj_Rebellion) of the 9th century, and [Basra](57580.md)'s role as a key port.[^cite_note-zanj-272] It is the most populous country in the [Arabian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_Plate).[^cite_note-geodatathe-265]

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Iraq)



Children in a village near the city of [Sulaymaniyah](653537.md)

The main languages spoken in Iraq are [Mesopotamian Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamian_Arabic_language) and [Kurdish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_language), followed by the [Iraqi Turkmen/Turkoman dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Turkmens#Language) of [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language), and the [Neo-Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Aramaic) languages (specifically [Chaldean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaldean_Neo-Aramaic) and [Assyrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_Neo-Aramaic) dialects).[^cite_note-273] Arabic and Kurdish are written with versions of the [Arabic script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_script). Since 2005, the Turkmen/Turkoman have switched from the Arabic script to the [Turkish alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_alphabet).[^cite_note-Shanks_2016_loc=57-274] In addition, the Neo-Aramaic languages use the [Syriac script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_script). Other smaller minority languages include [Mandaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandaic_language), [Shabaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabaki_language), [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_language), [Circassian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassian_language) and [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language).

Prior to the invasion in 2003, [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language) was the sole official language. Since the new [Constitution of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Iraq) was approved in 2005, both Arabic and [Kurdish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_language) are recognised (Article 4) as official languages of Iraq, while three other languages, [Turkmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Turkmens#Language), [Syriac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_language) and [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_language), are also recognised as [minority languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_languages). In addition, any region or province may declare other languages official if a majority of the population approves in a general referendum.[^cite_note-Constitution-43]

According to the [Constitution of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Iraq) (Article 4): The Arabic language and the Kurdish language are the two official languages of Iraq. The right of Iraqis to educate their children in their mother tongue, such as Turkmen, Syriac, and Armenian shall be guaranteed in government educational institutions in accordance with educational guidelines, or in any other language in private educational institutions.[^cite_note-Constitution-43]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Iraq)

See also: [Irreligion in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_Iraq)

Further information: [Islam in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Iraq), [Christianity in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Iraq), and [Judaism in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaism_in_Iraq)



Shrine in Karbala, showing use of [Arabesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabesque)

Religions in Iraq are dominantly [Abrahamic religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrahamic_religions).[^cite_note-IF-275] In 2020, the Association of Religion Data Archives (ARDA) estimated that 97% Iraqis followed Islam, with 61% being Shia and 35% Sunni.[^cite_note-276] An older estimate in 2015 by the CIA [World Factbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_World_Factbook) that reported between 90 and 95% of Iraqis followed [Islam](6037917.md), with 61–64% being Shia and 29–34% being Sunni. [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Iraq) accounted for 1%, and the rest (1-4%) practiced [Yazidism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazidism), [Mandaeism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandaeism), and other religions.[^cite_note-IF-275] In 2011, [Pew Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) estimated that 51% of Muslims in Iraq see themselves as [Shia](26961.md), 42% as [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni), while 5% as "just a Muslim".[^cite_note-277] Iraq is also home to two of the holiest places among the Shias – [Najaf](200512.md) and [Karbala](210957.md).[^cite_note-278] Shia Muslims are mostly concentrated in southern Iraq and in parts of north region and Baghdad. [Sunni Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Muslims) are found in the [Sunni Triangle region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Triangle), in cities such as [Ramadi](245655.md), [Tikrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tikrit) and [Fallujah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fallujah), where Sunnis make majority.

Religion in Iraq ([ARDA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Religion_Data_Archives) 2020 est.)

[^cite_note-279]

1. [Shia](26961.md) (61.2%)

2. [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni) (35.3%)

3. [Other Muslim](6037917.md) (0.84%)

4. [Christianity](5211.md) (0.48%)

5. [No religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion) (0.66%)

6. other (1.47%)

[Christianity in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Iraq) has its roots from the conception of the [Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_East) in the 5th century AD, predating the existence of Islam in the region of Iraq.[^cite_note-CNEWA-2024-280] Iraqi Christians are predominantly native [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_people) belonging to the [Ancient Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Church_of_the_East), [Assyrian Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_Church_of_the_East), [Chaldean Catholic Church](698821.md), [Syriac Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_Catholic_Church) and [Syriac Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_Orthodox_Church).[^cite_note-CNEWA-2024-280][^cite_note-Guardian-281] There is also a significant population of [Armenian Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Apostolic_Church) in Iraq who had fled [Turkey](11125639.md) during the [Armenian genocide](140376.md).[^cite_note-CNEWA-2024-280][^cite_note-Guardian-281] Christians numbered over 1.4 million in 1987 or 8% of the estimated population of 16.3 million and 550,000 in 1947 or 12% of the population of 4.6 millions.[^cite_note-282] After the 2003 [invasion of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Iraq), violence against Christians rose, with reports of abduction, torture, bombings, and killings.[^cite_note-besieged-283][^cite_note-euaa.europa.eu-2024-284][^cite_note-Guardian-281] The [post-2003 war](5043324.md) has displaced much of the [remaining Christian community from their homeland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_exodus_from_Iraq) as a result of ethnic and religious persecution at the hands of [Islamic extremists](8734632.md).[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286][^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288][^cite_note-Telegraph-1-289]

Iraq is home to one of the [oldest Jewish communities in the Middle East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Iraq) and the first Jewish diaspora.[^cite_note-Stone-2003-290] In 1948, the Jewish population was estimated at 200,000, although some sources suggest the population may have been even higher.[^cite_note-Stone-2003-290] After the establishment of [Israel](9282173.md) in 1948, Jews emigrated, [fleeing persecution in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_exodus_from_the_Muslim_world), while 100,000 of them remained.[^cite_note-291] By the time Saddam Hussein came to power, their population had reached 15,000.[^cite_note-Margit-2021-292][^cite_note-Amwaj-2024-293] Under his rule, the population dwindled—not due to persecution, but because the government lifted travel restrictions, allowing many Jews to emigrate abroad and visiting Iraq occasionally.[^cite_note-NYT-1971-294] At this point, around 1,500 Jews remained.[^cite_note-Shenon-1990-295] After 2003, fear among the Jewish community increased, leading to their further decline.[^cite_note-By-1998-296] Today, it is estimated that only around 400 Jews remain in Iraq.[^cite_note-ijao-2018-297] Iraq is home to over [250 Jewish sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Jewish_sites_in_Iraq).

There are also small [ethno-religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethno-religious) minority populations of [Mandaeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandaeans), [Shabaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabaks), [Yarsan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarsan) and [Yezidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yezidi) remaining.[^cite_note-euaa.europa.eu-2024-284] Prior to 2003 their numbers together may have been 2 million, the majority Yarsan, a non-Islamic religion with roots in pre-Islamic and pre-Christian religion.[^cite_note-euaa.europa.eu-2024-284] Yazidis are mostly concentrated around the [Sinjar Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinjar_Mountains).[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-euaa.europa.eu-2024-284] Mandaeans live primarily around Baghdad, Fallujah, Basra and [Hillah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillah).[^cite_note-Al-Saadi-2024-299][^cite_note-euaa.europa.eu-2024-284]

Religious freedom in Iraq is limited due to a risk of persecution or violence in case of religious conversion away from Islam.[^cite_note-k813-300]

### Diaspora and refugees

Main articles: [Refugees of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugees_of_Iraq) and [Assyrian exodus from Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_exodus_from_Iraq)

The dispersion of native Iraqis to other countries is known as the [Iraqi diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_diaspora). The [UN High Commission for Refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_High_Commission_for_Refugees) has estimated that nearly two million Iraqis fled the country after the [multinational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-National_Force_%E2%80%93_Iraq) invasion of Iraq in 2003.[^cite_note-301] The UN Refugee agency estimated in 2021 that 1.1 million were displaced within the country.[^cite_note-302] In 2007, the UN said that about 40% of Iraq's middle class was believed to have fled and that most had fled systematic persecution and had no desire to return.[^cite_note-303] Subsequently, the diaspora seemed to be returning, as security improved; the Iraqi government claimed that 46,000 refugees returned to their homes in October 2007 alone.[^cite_note-304]

In 2011, nearly 3 million Iraqis had been displaced, with 1.3 million within Iraq and 1.6 million in neighbouring countries, mainly Jordan and Syria.[^cite_note-305][^cite_note-BBC-2010-306][^cite_note-Sabah-2007-307] More than half of Iraqi Christians had fled the country since the US-led invasion.[^cite_note-BBC-2010-306][^cite_note-Sabah-2007-307] According to official [US Citizenship and Immigration Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Citizenship_and_Immigration_Services) statistics, 58,811 Iraqis had been granted refugee-status citizenship as of 25 May 2011.[^cite_note-308] After the start of the [Syrian Civil War](30741795.md) in 2011, numerous Iraqis in Syria returned to their native country.[^cite_note-309] To escape the [Syrian civil war](30741795.md), over 252,000 [Syrian refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugees_of_the_Syrian_civil_war) of varying ethnicities have fled to Iraq since 2012.[^cite_note-310]

### Health

Main article: [Health in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Iraq)

In 2010, spending on healthcare accounted for 6.84% of the country's GDP. In 2008, there were 6.96 physicians and 13.92 nurses per 10,000 inhabitants.[^cite_note-311] The life expectancy at birth was 68.49 years in 2010, or 65.13 years for males and 72.01 years for females.[^cite_note-312] This is down from a peak life expectancy of 71.31 years in 1996.[^cite_note-313] Officially, healthcare is free in Iraq.[^cite_note-314] However, years of wars, conflicts, and instability have left a deep impact of healthcare, just like other sectors of Iraq.

Iraq had developed a centralised free health care system in the 1970s using a hospital based, capital-intensive model of [curative care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curative_care).[^cite_note-315] The country depended on large-scale imports of medicines, medical equipment and even nurses, paid for with oil export income, according to a "Watching Brief" report issued jointly by the [UN Children's Fund](19698110.md) (UNICEF) and the [World Health Organization](33583.md) in July 2003.[^cite_note-corpwatch-316] Unlike other poorer countries, which focused on mass health care using primary care practitioners, Iraq developed a Westernised system of sophisticated hospitals with advanced medical procedures, provided by specialist physicians.[^cite_note-corpwatch-316] The UNICEF/WHO report noted that prior to 1990, 97% of urban dwellers and 71% of the rural population had access to free primary health care; just 2% of hospital beds were privately managed.[^cite_note-corpwatch-316]

In 2024, [Mohammed Shia' al-Sudani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Shia'_Al_Sudani) officially inaugurated Shaab General Hospital, Baghdad's first new general hospital in nearly 40 years.[^cite_note-IBN-2024-317] The 246-bed facility, which was a long-delayed project was completed under a collaborative management model, which boasts state-of-the-art infrastructure, with advanced medical equipment, and a full range of healthcare services according to Sudani.[^cite_note-IBN-2024-317] Minister of Health Salih Hasnawi highlighted the ministry's accomplishments over the past two years, including the construction of 13 new hospitals, three specialised centres, two burn units, and 25 kidney treatment centres in different governorates, while plans are in place to build 16 new hospitals, each with 100 beds, to be managed by qualified companies.[^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319] In the same year, the government launched the implementation of a joint operation and management programme for modern hospitals at the newly opened Najaf Teaching Hospital.[^cite_note-320]

### Education

Main article: [Education in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Iraq)



University students in Iraq, 2016

Before 1990 and later 2003, Iraq already had an advanced and successful education system.[^cite_note-de_Santisteban-2005-321] However, it has now been "de-developing" in its educational success.[^cite_note-de_Santisteban-2005-321] During his rule, Saddam turned Iraq into a leading centre of higher education.[^cite_note-de_Santisteban-2005-321] Since the implementation of the MDGs, education has shown improvement in Iraq.[^cite_note-de_Santisteban-2005-321] Enrollment numbers nearly doubled from 2000 to 2012, reaching six million students.[^cite_note-unicef.org-322] By 2015–2016, around 9.2 million children were attending school, with a steady annual increase of 4.1% in enrollment rates.[^cite_note-unicef.org-322]

However, the rapid increase in primary education students has strained the system.[^cite_note-unicef.org-322] Education receives only 5.7% of government spending, leading to a lack of investment in schools and poor educational rankings in the region.[^cite_note-unicef.org-322] UNICEF found that funding has been wasted, resulting in increasing dropout and repetition rates.[^cite_note-unicef.org-322] Dropout rates range from 1.5% to 2.5%, with girls being affected more due to economic or family reasons. Repetition rates have reached almost 17%, causing a loss of approximately 20% of education funding in 2014–2015.[^cite_note-unicef.org-322]

Regional disparities greatly impact enrollment rates for children in primary education in Iraq.[^cite_note-unicef.org-322] Conflict-ridden areas like Saladin Governorate have seen over 90% of school-age children out of school due to the conversion of schools into shelters or military bases.[^cite_note-unicef.org-322] Limited resources strain the education system, hindering access to education.[^cite_note-unicef.org-322] However, efforts have been made to reopen closed schools, with success seen in Mosul, where over 380,000 children are back in school.[^cite_note-unicef.org-322] Access to education varies depending on location, and there are disparities between boys and girls.[^cite_note-unicef.org-322]

In 2024, the government inaugurated 790 new schools across the country, as part of a framework agreement with China to build 1,000 schools. This initiative aims to address overcrowding and the issue of triple shifts in schools, which have been exacerbated by the destruction caused by years of conflict.[^cite_note-Shafaq_News-2025-323] Many schools have had to operate multiple shifts, sometimes giving students as little as four hours of learning per day, which negatively affects educational outcomes.[^cite_note-Shafaq_News-2025-323][^cite_note-Salem-2024-324] The school construction project stems from a 2021 agreement between the Iraqi and Chinese governments to build 1,000 schools. Additionally, the Iraqi Prime Minister announced that the [Iraq Development Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Development_Fund_\(Iraq\)) will soon collaborate with the private sector to build 400 more schools, addressing the current shortage of over 8,000 schools in the country.[^cite_note-Shafaq_News-2025-323][^cite_note-Salem-2024-324]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Iraq)

Iraq's culture has a deep heritage that extends back in time to ancient Mesopotamian culture. Iraq has one of the longest written traditions in the world including [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture), [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature), [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music), [dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance), [painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Painting), [weaving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weaving), [pottery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pottery), [calligraphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calligraphy), [stonemasonry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonemasonry) and [metalworking](266443.md). The culture of Iraq or Mesopotamia is one of the world's oldest [cultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture) histories and is considered one of the most influential cultures in the world.

Mesopotamian legacy went on to influence and shape the civilisations of the [Old World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_World) in different ways such as inventing [writing system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuneiform), [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_mathematics), [time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time), [calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonian_calendar), [astrology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonian_astrology) and the [law code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Code_of_Ur-Nammu).[^cite_note-Encyclopædia_Britannica-325][^cite_note-World_History_Encyclopedia-326] Iraq is home to diverse ethnic groups that have each contributed in different ways to the country's long and rich heritage. The country is known for its poets, architects, painters and sculptors, who are among the best in the region, some of them being world-class. Iraq is known for producing fine [handicrafts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handicrafts), including [rugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpet) and [carpets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpets).

### Art

Main articles: [Art of Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_of_Mesopotamia) and [Iraqi art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_art)



[Wasiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahya_ibn_Mahmud_al-Wasiti)'s illustrations served as an inspiration for the modern Baghdad art movement in the 20th-century.

[^cite_note-327]

There were several interconnected traditions of art in ancient Iraq. The [Abbasid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid) Dynasty developed in the [Abbasid Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Caliphate) between 750 and 945, primarily in its heartland of [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia). The Abbasids were influenced mainly by [Mesopotamian art traditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_of_Mesopotamia) and later influenced [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_architecture) as well as Central Asian styles. Between the 8th and 13th centuries during the Abbasid period, pottery achieved a high level of sophistication, calligraphy began to be used to decorate the surface of decorative objects and illuminated manuscripts, particularly [Q'ranic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) texts became more complex and stylised. Iraq's first art school was established during this period, allowing artisans and crafts to flourish.[^cite_note-328]

At the height of the Abbasid period, in the late 12th century, a stylistic movement of manuscript illustration and calligraphy emerged. Now known as the *[Baghdad School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baghdad_School)*, this movement of Islamic art was characterised by representations of everyday life and the use of highly expressive faces rather than the stereotypical characters that had been used in the past.[^cite_note-Online-329]

### Architecture

Main article: [Architecture of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Iraq)



[Zaha Hadid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaha_Hadid) (1950–2016), an acclaimed architect

The architecture of Iraq has a long history, encompassing several distinct cultures and spanning a period from the 10th millennium BC and features both the [Mesopotamian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Mesopotamia) and [Abbasid architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_architecture).[^cite_note-ARCADE-2017-330] Baghdad and Mosul have plethora of cultural and heritage buildings. There are numerous [historic mosques in Baghdad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mosques_in_Baghdad) and Basra, [old churches in Mosul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_churches_and_monasteries_in_Nineveh) and [synagogues in Baghdad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Jewish_sites_in_Iraq).[^cite_note-ARCADE-2017-330] Modern prominent architects include [Zaha Hadid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaha_Hadid), [Basil Bayati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basil_Al_Bayati), [Rifat Chadirji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rifat_Chadirji) and [Hisham N. Ashkouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hisham_N._Ashkouri) among others.[^cite_note-ARCADE-2017-330]

The capital, Ninus or [Nineveh](21699.md), was taken by the [Medes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medes) under [Cyaxares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyaxares), and some 200 years after [Xenophon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xenophon) passed over its site, then mere mounds of earth. It remained buried until 1845, when Botta and [Layard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austen_Henry_Layard) discovered the ruins of the Assyrian cities. The principal remains are those of [Khorsabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khorsabad), 16 km (10 mi) northeast of [Mosul](200475.md); of Nimroud, supposed to be the ancient Calah; and of Kouyunjik, in all probability the ancient Nineveh. In these cities are found fragments of several great buildings which seem to have been palace-temples. They were constructed chiefly of [sun-dried bricks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mudbrick), and all that remains of them is the lower part of the walls, decorated with sculpture and paintings, portions of the pavements, a few indications of the elevation, and some works connected with the drainage.

In recent years, modern buildings include shopping malls and high-rise towers.[^cite_note-\:0-331] Iraq was of the first countries along with Egypt, to adopt mall culture in the Arab world and the Middle East.[^cite_note-\:0-331] Al-Adil Shopping Center (formerly Ozdi Pak) in Baghdad was the second mall in the region after Egypt.[^cite_note-\:0-331]

&#x20;Important cultural institutions in the capital include the [Iraqi National Symphony Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_National_Symphony_Orchestra) – rehearsals and performances were briefly interrupted during the [occupation of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Iraq_\(2003%E2%80%932011\)) but have since returned to normal.[^cite_note-332] The National Theatre of Iraq was looted during the 2003 invasion, but efforts are underway to restore it. The live theatre scene received a boost during the 1990s when UN sanctions limited the import of foreign films. As many as 30 cinemas were reported to have been converted to live stages, producing a wide range of comedies and dramatic productions.



Facade of Temple at [Hatra](200437.md) near Mosul was declared [World Heritage Site](44940.md) by [UNESCO](21786641.md) in 1985

[^cite_note-333]

Institutions offering cultural education in Baghdad include the Academy of Music, Institute of Fine Arts and the [Music and Ballet School Baghdad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_and_Ballet_school_Baghdad). Baghdad also features a number of museums including the [National Museum of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Iraq) – which houses the world's largest and finest collection of artefacts and relics of [Ancient Iraqi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Iraq) civilisations; some of which were [stolen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeological_looting_in_Iraq) during the [occupation of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Iraq_\(2003%E2%80%932011\)). On 2021, it was announced that Iraq had reclaimed about 17,000 looted artefacts, which was considered to be the biggest repatriation.[^cite_note-334]

### Literature

Main articles: [Akkadian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkadian_literature), [Sumerian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumerian_literature), and [Iraqi literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_literature)



An Akkadian inscription

The literature in Iraq is often referred to as "Mesopotamian literature" due to the flourishing of various civilisations as a result of the mixture of these cultures and has been called Mesopotamian or Babylonian literature in allusion to the geographical territory that such cultures occupied in the [Middle East](19323.md) between the banks of the Tigris and Euphrates rivers.[^cite_note-335] The Sumerian literature was unique because it does not belong to any known linguistic root. Its appearance began with symbols of the things denoting it, then it turned with time to the cuneiform line on tablets. The literature during this time were mainly about mythical and epic texts dealing with creation issues, the emergence of the world, the gods, descriptions of the heavens, and the lives of heroes in the wars that broke out between the nomads and the urbanites. They also deal with religious teachings, moral advice, astrology, legislation, and history. One of which was the [Epic of Gilgamesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epic_of_Gilgamesh), which is regarded as the earliest surviving notable literature.[^cite_note-336]

During the Abbasid Caliphate, the [House of Wisdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Wisdom) in [Baghdad](4492.md), which was a public academy and intellectual fulcrum, hosted numerous scholars and writers. A number of stories in *[One Thousand and One Nights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Thousand_and_One_Nights)* feature famous Abbasid figures.[^cite_note-337] Iraq has various medieval poets, most remarkably [Hariri of Basra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Hariri_of_Basra), [Mutanabbi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Mutanabbi), [Abu Nuwas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Nuwas), and [Al-Jahiz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Jahiz). In modern times, various languages are used in Iraqi literature including Arabic, [Neo-Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Aramaic_languages), [Kurdish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_languages) and [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language), although the Arabic literature remains the most influential literature. Notably poets include [Jawahiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Mahdi_al-Jawahiri), [Safa Khulusi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safa_Khulusi) and [Dunya Mikhail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunya_Mikhail).

### Music

Main article: [Music of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Iraq)



[Kadim Al Sahir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kadim_Al_Sahir) known as "**The** **Caesar"** of Arabic songs. Considered as one of the most successful singers in the history of the [Arab World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_World).

Iraq is known primarily for its rich [maqam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_maqam) [heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historic_site) which has been passed down orally by the masters of the maqam in an unbroken chain of transmission leading up to the present. The [Iraqi maqam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_maqam) is considered to be the most noble and perfect form of maqam. Al-maqam al-Iraqi is the collection of sung poems written either in one of the 16 meters of classical Arabic or in Iraqi dialect (Zuhayri).[^cite_note-338] This form of art is recognised by UNESCO as "an intangible heritage of humanity".[^cite_note-339]

Early in the 20th century, many of the most prominent musicians in Iraq were [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jew).[^cite_note-Kojaman-340] In 1936, [Iraq Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Radio?action=edit\&redlink=1) was established with an ensemble made up entirely of [Jews](25955086.md), with the exception of the percussion player.[^cite_note-Kojaman-340] At the nightclubs of Baghdad, ensembles consisted of oud, qanun and two percussionists, while the same format with a [ney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ney) and [cello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cello) were used on the radio.[^cite_note-Kojaman-340]

The most famous singer of the 1930s–1940s was perhaps [Salima Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salima_Pasha) (later Salima Murad).[^cite_note-Kojaman-340][^cite_note-Manasseh-341] The respect and adoration for Pasha were unusual at the time since public performance by women was considered shameful.[^cite_note-Kojaman-340] The most famous early composer from Iraq was [Ezra Aharon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ezra_Aharon?action=edit\&redlink=1), an [oud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oud) player, while the most prominent instrumentalist was [Yusuf Za'arur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusuf_Za'arur). Za'arus formed the official ensemble for the Iraqi radio station and were responsible for introducing the cello and ney into the traditional ensemble.[^cite_note-Kojaman-340]

### Media

Main articles: [Media of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Iraq), [Television in Iraq](231027.md), [Cinema of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Iraq), and [List of Iraqi films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Iraqi_films)



An image showing a page from Iraq newspaper, 5 June 1920

Iraq was home to the second television station in the Middle East, which began during the 1950s. As part of a plan to help Iraq modernise, English telecommunications company [Pye Limited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pye_Ltd.) built and commissioned a television broadcast station in the capital city of Baghdad.[^cite_note-342]

After the end of the full state control in 2003, there was a period of significant growth in the broadcast media in Iraq.[^cite_note-343] By 2003, according to a [BBC](19344654.md) report, there were 20 radio stations from 0.15 to 17 television stations owned by Iraqis, and 200 Iraqi newspapers owned and operated.

Iraqi media expert and author of a number of reports on this subject, Ibrahim Al Marashi, identifies four stages of the US invasion of Iraq in 2003 where they had been taking the steps that have significant effects on the way for the later of the Iraqi media since then. Stages are: pre-invasion preparation, and the war and the actual choice of targets, the first post-war period, and a growing insurgency and hand over power to the Iraqi Interim Government (IIG) and Prime Minister [Iyad Allawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iyad_Allawi).[^cite_note-344]

### Cuisine

Main article: [Iraqi cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_cuisine)



[Dolma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolma), a popular Iraqi dish

Iraqi cuisine can be traced back some 10,000 years – to the [Sumerians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumer), [Akkadians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkadian_Empire), [Babylonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonia), [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyria) and [Ancient Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire).[^cite_note-Salloum-345] [Tablets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clay_tablet) found in ancient ruins in Iraq show recipes prepared in the temples during religious festivals – the first cookbooks in the world.[^cite_note-Salloum-345] [Ancient Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Iraq), or *[Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia)*, was home to many sophisticated and highly advanced civilisations, in all fields of knowledge – including the culinary arts.[^cite_note-Salloum-345] However, it was in the [medieval era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age) when Baghdad was the capital of the [Abbasid Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Caliphate) that the Iraqi kitchen reached its zenith.[^cite_note-Salloum-345] Today the cuisine of Iraq reflects this rich inheritance as well as strong influences from the culinary traditions of neighbouring [Turkey](11125639.md), [Iran](14653.md) and the [Greater Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Syria) area.[^cite_note-Salloum-345]

Some characteristic ingredients of Iraqi cuisine include – vegetables such as [aubergine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aubergine), [tomato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomato), [okra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Okra), [onion](51258.md), [potato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potato), [courgette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Courgette), [garlic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garlic), [peppers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bell_pepper) and [chilli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chili_pepper), cereals such as [rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice), [bulgur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgur) [wheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat) and [barley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barley), pulses and legumes such as [lentils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lentils), [chickpeas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chickpeas) and [cannellini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannellini#White_beans), fruits such as [dates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Date_Palm#Dates), [raisins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raisin), [apricots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apricot), [figs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_fig), [grapes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grape), [melon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melon), [pomegranate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pomegranate) and [citrus fruits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citrus), especially [lemon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lemon) and [lime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lime_\(fruit\)).[^cite_note-Salloum-345]

Similarly with other countries of [Western Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Asia), chicken and especially lamb are the favourite meats. Most dishes are served with rice – usually [Basmati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basmati), grown in the [marshes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamian_Marshes) of southern Iraq.[^cite_note-Salloum-345] [Bulgur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgur) wheat is used in many dishes, having been a staple in the country since the days of the [Ancient Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyria).[^cite_note-Salloum-345]

### Sport

Main article: [Sport in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Iraq)



[Basra International Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basra_International_Stadium)

[Football](10568.md) is the most popular sport in Iraq. [Basketball](3921.md), [swimming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swimming_\(sport\)), [weightlifting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_weightlifting), [bodybuilding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodybuilding), [boxing](4243.md), [kick boxing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kick_boxing) and [tennis](29773.md) are also popular sports.

The [Iraq Football Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Football_Association) is the governing body of football in Iraq, controlling the [Iraq national football team](1039652.md) and the [Iraq Stars League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Stars_League). It was founded in 1948, and has been a member of [FIFA](11049.md) since 1950 and the [Asian Football Confederation](715249.md) since 1971. Iraq were champions of the [2007 AFC Asian Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_AFC_Asian_Cup), and they participated in the [1986 FIFA World Cup](157231.md), the [2009 FIFA Confederations Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_FIFA_Confederations_Cup) and the [2026 FIFA World Cup](36581929.md).

The constitution establishes Islam as the official religion and states no law may be enacted contradicting the "established provisions of Islam." It provides for freedom of religious belief and practice for all individuals, including Muslims, Christians, Yezidis, and Sabean-Mandeans, but does not explicitly mention followers of other religions or atheists.

[^cite_note-6]: &#x20;["Iraq"](https://web.archive.org/web/20260118091316/https://www.cia.gov/the-world-factbook/countries/iraq/). Archived from [the original](https://www.cia.gov/the-world-factbook/countries/iraq/) on 18 January 2026.

[^cite_note-8]: &#x20;["Without a Federation Council, State-building in Iraq remains elusive | MERI"](https://www.meri-k.org/publication/without-a-federal-council-state-building-in-iraq-remains-elusive/). *[www.meri-k.org](http://www.meri-k.org/)*. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20260405030148/https://www.meri-k.org/publication/without-a-federal-council-state-building-in-iraq-remains-elusive/) from the original on 5 April 2026. Retrieved 26 March 2025. The problem here does not lie in the topic, but in the fact that Iraq's shaky Parliament is enjoying a monopoly in the country's supreme legislative body and not subject to any form of checks and balances. Iraq was meant to have a Federation Council, an upper chamber, that can play a vital role in the country's democracy, state-building and rule-of-law. This Council was mandated in Articles 48 and 65 of the 2005 Constitution, and was meant to be created during the term of the first Parliament (2006-2010).

[^cite_note-9]: &#x20;["Can the Federation Council help address Iraq's ongoing 'state' crisis? - Kalam"](https://kalam.chathamhouse.org/articles/can-the-federation-council-help-address-iraqs-ongoing-state-crisis/). 13 March 2025. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20250328061324/https://kalam.chathamhouse.org/articles/can-the-federation-council-help-address-iraqs-ongoing-state-crisis/) from the original on 28 March 2025. Retrieved 26 March 2025. Article 65 of Iraq's 2005 Constitution calls for the establishment of a second legislative chamber known as the Federation Council (FC). However, this article remains dormant, awaiting 'to be regulated by law' – just like many other suspended constitutional provisions.

[^cite_note-10]: &#x20;Chomani, Kamal (8 November 2018). ["Iraq's Missing Federation Council"](https://timep.org/2018/11/08/iraqs-missing-federation-council/). *The Tahrir Institute for Middle East Policy -*. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20250706212105/https://timep.org/2018/11/08/iraqs-missing-federation-council/) from the original on 6 July 2025. Retrieved 26 March 2025. The Iraqi parliament's upper house, constitutionally defined as the Federation Council, has never been established. Establishing the Federation Council would be a step forward in Iraq's democratization and ability to resolve disputes.
